## Chapter III.

He says that only the **Disciples of Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy, from whose name the "Hermetic arts" are derived.</note> are capable of understanding the great things he has just taught, and of truly knowing the foundations of Nature, because they are like the **apes of the Creator** <note>original: "singes du Créateur." This is a reference to the concept of *Simia Naturae* (the Ape of Nature), suggesting that the alchemist does not invent new laws, but perfectly imitates God’s natural processes in the lab.</note> in their **physical work**; that like Him, they create their **chaos**; like Him, they separate the **Light** from the **Darkness**; they create, like Him, their **Firmament** separating the **waters from the waters**; they create their **Sun** and their **Moon**, and finally perfectly accomplish the entire work of **Creation**.

He says that all of this is achieved from a single **corpuscle** <term>corpuscle: a minute particle or small body of matter</term> where there is nothing but **dregs** <note>original: "feces." In early chemistry, this refers to the solid residue or waste matter left after a process like distillation.</note> and abomination, from which one extracts a certain dark and **mercurial humidity** that contains within itself everything necessary to the **Philosopher** <note>In this context, "Philosopher" refers specifically to an alchemist or "Philosopher by Fire."</note>; and he applies to this the famous passage which says that ***Mercury is everything that the Sages seek.***

<summary>The author explains how alchemists act as "imitators of God" by recreating the stages of Genesis—separating light from dark and waters from waters—within their laboratory work. This process begins with a humble, seemingly worthless substance from which the essential "Philosophical Mercury" is extracted.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Ape of Nature, Creation, Chaos, Firmament, Mercury, alchemy as imitation of God, corpuscule, dregs, Sages</keywords>

### In Chapter IV.

He blames those who, notwithstanding the express prohibitions of the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical literature, "Philosophers" refers to the masters of the art or the authors of classical alchemical texts.</note> against using **common mercury** <term>common mercury: ordinary liquid quicksilver (quicksilver vulgaire), which alchemists argued was "dead" and unsuitable for the Work compared to their "living" philosophical mercury</term>, persist in working upon it. By the very fact that they [the Philosophers] forbid it, he explains the reason for their error; and he warns us that one must work upon a body created by Nature, in which she has herself joined together **Sulfur** and **Mercury**, which the **Artist** <note>original: "l'Artiſte." This refers to the alchemist as a skilled craftsman who assists nature.</note> must separate, and once separated, purify them, and join them together once more. He calls that body ***Iliaster hyle*** <note>original: "Illiaſtr hylé." A term popularized by Paracelsus, combining the Latin *astrum* (star) and the Greek *hyle* (matter) to describe primordial, chaotic matter.</note> or **chaos**.

### In Chapter VI.

It treats of the **seal of Hermes** <term>seal of Hermes: the origin of the term "hermetic seal," referring to a method of airtight sealing for a glass vessel, often involving melting the glass neck to ensure no vapors escape</term>, not the one commonly understood, but the **Philosophical** one; he says that there is skill <note>original: "industrie." ## CANTO II.

### *On the first Chapter.*

HE rebukes those who work with Gold, on the basis that within Gold are the seeds of Gold <note>The alchemical theory of "metallic vegetation" held that metals grew in the earth like plants. Therefore, to make gold, one needed its "seed."</note>; & shows that it is a subject of too strong a bond <note>original: "liaison." In alchemy, this refers to the tight chemical or spiritual union of a metal's components, which makes it resistant to change.</note>, which because of this can be altered & corrupted <note>In alchemy, "corruption" is a necessary stage of decay (putrefaction) that must happen before a substance can be reborn or "transmuted" into something higher.</note> only with great difficulty, & he advises that instead of attaching oneself to the fruit, one should take the root of the tree.

He condemns those who, having taken Gold for the seed, take common Mercury <note>original: "Mercure vulgaire." The everyday quicksilver found in shops, which alchemists argued lacked the "vital spark" required for the Great Work.</note> for the solvent, or for the earth in which it must be sown, & the reason he gives for this is that neither the one nor the other has within them an external agent; Gold for having been stripped of it by decoction <term>Decoction: The process of refining or "ripening" a substance through steady heat. **fatness of Sulfur** <note>original: "graisse du Souphre." In alchemical theory, this refers to the oily, combustible principle of sulfur that acts as a binding agent.</note>, it joins with it, & from their union is formed a **glutinous substance**, or **unformed mass**, upon which the **vapor** spread throughout these cavernous places acts by means of the **Sulfur** that it contains within itself; from this, **perfect metals** <note>Typically gold and silver, which alchemists believed had reached their full "maturity."</note> are formed if the place & the vapor are pure; & **imperfect** if, on the contrary, the place & the vapor are impure; & they are called imperfect or non-perfect for not having received their entire perfection through **decoction** <term>Decoction: The natural "cooking" or maturation process within the earth, where steady subterranean heat refines base minerals into noble metals over centuries.</term>.

Regarding **Mercury**, he gives the reason why it does not have an **external agent** with it, & shows that this comes from the fact that during the rising of the **double vapor**, the **commotion** is so great & so sudden that it causes the **spirit** or **agent** to evaporate, much like what happens during the **fusion** <note>original: "fusion," the process of melting metals.</note> of metals, so that the **material part** alone remains deprived of its **male** or **sulfurous agent** <note>In the "Mercury-Sulfur theory" of metals, sulfur was viewed as the active, formative "male" seed, while mercury was the passive, "female" matter.</note>, which is why it can never be trans- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "transformed" or "transmuted."</note>

->Summary<-

Sages who know how to construct and purify it, that it is very dry, that it is in a continual movement and seeks only to corrupt, and to bring things from potential into reality <note>original: "de puissance en acte," an Aristotelian concept where a thing's potentiality is transformed into its actualized state.</note>; and that it is this fire, finally, which, encountering solid places within the mines, circulates in the form of vapor over its matter and dissolves it; he says that it can be recognized by the fact that it encloses itself within sulfurous excrements <note>Refers to the impurities or "dross" that must be purged from the raw mineral.</note>, and clothes itself in a saline garment; he adds that this fire, because of its extreme dryness <term>dryness: "siccité," one of the four primary qualities (hot, cold, wet, dry) that governed the behavior of elements in alchemical theory</term>, needs to be moistened to better penetrate the **feminine sperm** <note>The passive, receptive principle of the matter, often identified with Mercury, which must be "fertilized" by the active masculine principle (Sulfur).</note>, and that one must fish for it with a subtle net and by a certain means suited for that purpose; but to succeed in this, one must be well acquainted with the **sympathies** <note>The "sympathies of things" refers to the belief that natural objects are connected by hidden occult relationships and attractions.</note> of things and be well-versed in **natural magic**.

### *In Chapter IV.*

He says that the whole secret of the **Art** <note>The "Art" or "Great Work" (Ars Magna) refers to the chemical and spiritual process of alchemy.</note> consists in assisting **Nature** in

<header>of this Treatise.</header>

the administration of fire, not only external but internal; the external to act, and the internal to shorten the **work** <note>The "Work" (or Opus) is the alchemical process of transforming base matter into the Philosopher's Stone.</note> by the addition of a more **digested sulfur** <term>digested: in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to mature it, similar to how food is processed in the stomach.</term>.

He passes from there to the explanation of the **Philosophical fires** <note>Philosophical fires are not literal flames, but rather the internal energies and chemical properties of the substances themselves.</note>, which are the natural, the unnatural, and the against-nature. He says that the natural is the **masculine fire** <note>The active, fertilizing principle, often associated with Sulfur.</note> or the primary agent; that the unnatural is the **feminine fire** <note>The passive, receptive principle, often associated with Mercury.</note>, or the natural solvent, nourishing and taking the form of **white smoke** <note>Likely referring to the mercurial vapors that rise during heating.</note>, which vanishes easily when it is in this form if one does not take great care. He notes that it is almost incomprehensible, although through **Philosophical sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then condensed back into a purified solid.</term> it becomes physical and radiant. Regarding the fire against-nature, he says that it is the one which corrupts the compound and which has the power to unbind what Nature had strongly bound.

### Regarding Chapter V.

He discusses the **unity of matter** <note>The alchemical belief that all physical things share a single, fundamental source called the *Prima Materia*.</note>, and maintains that it is unique, not only when considered as an abstract concept, but also insofar as it is the **subject** <note>The "subject" refers to the raw starting material chosen for the alchemical process.</note> that the **Artist** <note>The "Artist" is a respectful term for the alchemist, viewing the transformation of matter as a high craft or art form.</note> must take in hand.

He argues against the **plurality of matters** <note>He warns against using a complex mixture of many different substances, which was a common mistake for "puffers" or unskilled alchemists.</note>, because Art is not capable of knowing the proportions nor the weights of [complex] things.

He says that this subject is found everywhere, but that it must nevertheless be sought within the **metallic nature**, where it is found more easily than elsewhere.

He says that there are several matters of this sort, but that one must be preferred above the others—namely, the most mature, the cleanest, and the easiest—but one must take care above all that the **metallic essence** be present, not only in **potency** <term>potency: the Aristotelian concept of *potentia*, referring to a latent power or potential that has not yet been realized.</term>, but also in **act** <term>act: the concept of *actus*, referring to a quality that is fully realized, active, and manifest in the physical world.</term>, and that there be a **metallic splendor** <note>original: "splendeur metallique"; likely referring to the characteristic luster or "inner light" of a high-quality mineral or metal.</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: unity of matter, Artist, plurality of matters, metallic nature, metallic essence, metallic splendor</note>

<summary>This page summarizes the arguments for starting the alchemical work with a single, specific substance found within the realm of metals rather than a mixture of materials. It emphasizes selecting a material where the metallic essence is already active and visible rather than just a dormant potential.</summary>
<keywords>unity of matter, Prima Materia, metallic nature, potency and act, metallic splendor, alchemical subject</keywords>

of this Treatise. 327

He says that in truth everything is contained within this **subject** <note>The "subject" refers to the raw starting material or *Prima Materia* used in the alchemical process.</note>, but that one must nevertheless assist **Nature** <note>The alchemist views themselves as a "midwife" to nature, using art to accelerate and perfect the natural growth of metals.</note> so that the work might be better and more quickly accomplished, and this by a double means which must be well understood.

He says that this subject is **vile** <note>A common alchemical trope: the "Great Work" begins with a substance that is common, cheap, or even repulsive to the ignorant, such as earth, lead, or waste.</note> and initially has no elegance in itself; that if some say it is marketable, they are considering its outward species, but fundamentally it is not sold because it is only useful for our work. He assures us that it often falls into the hands of many people who reject it out of sheer ignorance, as happened to himself.

### *Regarding Chapter VII.*

He forbids not only a plurality of matters, but also the division of a single matter into two parts to reunite them later, and claims that this disturbs the **weights of Nature** <note>The "weights of Nature" refer to the natural proportions and inherent balance of elements within a substance.</note>, which it is not within the power of **Art** <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the alchemical craft or the human intervention in natural processes.</note> to restore.

### *Regarding Chapter VIII.*

He rebukes those who work on gums, resins, salts, **strong waters** <term>Eaux fortes: Historically, this refers to corrosive acids like nitric acid used to dissolve metals.</term>, **vitriols** <term>Vitriols: Sulfate minerals (like copper or iron sulfate) often used as starting materials in early chemistry.</term>, common **Sulfur**, and common **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argent vif vulgaire"; alchemists often distinguished between "vulgar" or common substances sold by merchants and their own "philosophical" versions.</note>, on **Antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid that was a popular subject for alchemical experimentation.</note> and even on metals themselves; ordering alway-

### *Regarding Chapter IX.*

He treats the subject of weights, and teaches that they are achieved only through the path of **attraction** <note>In alchemy, "attraction" refers to the natural affinity or "magnetic" pull between elements that brings them into balance.</note> within the work, and that this is properly the perfect equality of the Elements, such that one does not dominate the others.

### *Regarding Chapter X.*

He speaks only of the miraculous virtues of the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and conferring longevity.</note>, and shows in passing that one can, by its means, make glass malleable <note>The "malleable glass" (vitrum flexile) was a legendary lost invention often associated in the Renaissance with the perfection of alchemical knowledge.</note>.

## TO THE MOST INGENIOUS MAN

# MASTER PANURGE

### THE HERMETIC LEAGUE

->To fare well. <note>original Greek: εὖ ἐργάζεσϑαι (eu ergazesthai), a traditional greeting wishing success in one's actions or health.</note><-

We rejoice greatly that, at long last, there has been found in your otherwise most learned France <note>original Latin: Gallia</note>, a man who has achieved an understanding with us of the most subtle mysteries—or perhaps the delusions—of the ancient Hermetic art <note>The "Hermetic art" refers to alchemy and the esoteric philosophy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. Indeed, most ingenious **PANURGE**, we suspect this of you for the reason that we see our enigmatic games have found favor with you. Whether, however, you truly possess the actual knowledge of the ancient Matter <term>Materia: the "First Matter" (Materia Prima), the elusive raw substance required to begin the alchemical Great Work</term> itself, we do not yet know for certain; for you have brought forth nothing from your own perception, but have only praised our own works, which

### LETTER

to anyone, even if they do not penetrate those same mysteries, it is permitted to offer praise. Furthermore, we lament your fate and ours alike, because it has not yet been our fortune to be approached by a true and actual Adept of the Art <term>Artis Adepto: An "Adept" is someone who has supposedly achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy, successfully creating the Philosophers' Stone.</term>, and by him to be made more certain that these things are not mere inventions of a subtle mind and phantasms, which authors have claimed concerning the effects of that Hermetic Matter <note>original Latin: Materiæ Hermeticæ</note>. Perhaps these authors, led by the same reasoning as we—from mere conjectures and intellectual discourse—have filled the world with such grand promises. For why is there nowhere found in the Treasuries <note>original: Gazophylaciis, referring to royal storehouses or cabinets of curiosities</note> of Kings, or of other curious collectors, any specimen of malleable glass <note>original: vitro malleabili; a legendary material that could be hammered like metal without shattering, often claimed as a byproduct of alchemical mastery.</note>, which the Hermeticists nonetheless publicly boast can be made by their Sophic Stone <term>Lapide suo Sophico: The "Philosophers' Stone," the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and composing the Elixir of Life.</term>? Why are there no long-lived old men to be found, who, by that universal medicine which they call the Tree of Life <note>original: Arborem vitæ</note>, have extended their age beyond their hundredth year?

It is not, therefore, most Illustrious Man, a reason for you to congratulate yourself and us on the knowledge of that Matter, to which no—

### LETTER

[No one] provides the testimony of living Adepts <term>Adepti: In alchemy, an "Adept" is a master who has successfully completed the "Great Work," supposedly achieving the power to transmute metals or grant long life.</term>, even if we have dealt with them through threats or prayer—though they perhaps exist nowhere on earth. For this reason, we remain firmly resolved that, after a short time, we shall publish all those supposed *Secrets of the Art in Print*; so that so many ingenious men may no longer have the opportunity to deceive themselves and others with those counterfeit, though clever, speculations.

For this reason, we ask you, most ingenious Panurge, that you would not find it troublesome to make this intention of ours—expressed in our *Proclamatory Letters* <note>original Latin: Epistolis Buccinatoriis; literally "trumpet-blowing letters," used here to mean public manifestos or heraldic announcements.</note> and in this very letter—known to men deeply learned in *Hermetic Philosophy* (not those common *Alchemists*, but those who would rather be *Master Craftsmen* than merely be thought of as such), if indeed any such *Cosmopolitans* <note>original Latin: Cosmopolitæ; a term used by traveling alchemists to indicate they were "citizens of the world," often to avoid local persecution or to claim a higher, universal status.</note> inhabit your French *Athens* <note>A common scholarly reference to Paris, which was considered the center of intellectual and artistic life in the 17th century.</note>.

To this end, we here depict the *Symbol* of our knowledge of Hermetics, expressed in those words and lines which we believe represent the mind of the first discoverer, *Trismegistus* <term>Trismegisti: Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy, astrology, and magic is attributed.</term>.

<summary>The authors express frustration at the lack of living alchemical masters and threaten to publish secret knowledge to stop the spread of false theories. They ask Panurge to share this news with true scholars in Paris and prepare to present a symbolic diagram of their wisdom.</summary>
